=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array points of size n and an integer m. There is another array gameScore of size n, where gameScore[i] represents the score achieved at the i^th game. Initially, gameScore[i] == 0 for all i.
You start at index -1, which is outside the array (before the first position at index 0). You can make at most m moves. In each move, you can either:

Increase the index by 1 and add points[i] to gameScore[i].
Decrease the index by 1 and add points[i] to gameScore[i].

Note that the index must always remain within the bounds of the array after the first move.
Return the maximum possible minimum value in gameScore after at most m moves.
 
Example 1:

Input: points = [2,4], m = 3
Output: 4
Explanation:
Initially, index i = -1 and gameScore = [0, 0].



Move
Index
gameScore




Increase i
0
[2, 0]


Increase i
1
[2, 4]


Decrease i
0
[4, 4]



The minimum value in gameScore is 4, and this is the maximum possible minimum among all configurations. Hence, 4 is the output.

Example 2:

Input: points = [1,2,3], m = 5
Output: 2
Explanation:
Initially, index i = -1 and gameScore = [0, 0, 0].



Move
Index
gameScore




Increase i
0
[1, 0, 0]


Increase i
1
[1, 2, 0]


Decrease i
0
[2, 2, 0]


Increase i
1
[2, 4, 0]


Increase i
2
[2, 4, 3]



The minimum value in gameScore is 2, and this is the maximum possible minimum among all configurations. Hence, 2 is the output.

 
Constraints:

2 <= n == points.length <= 5 * 10^4
1 <= points[i] <= 10^6
1 <= m <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

    def maxScore(self, points: List[int], m: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Now derive efficient min_sum_E for given a_i.

Let's define variables E_i for edges 0..n-2. For each vertex i, constraint:
For i=0:
E_0 + 1 + e_0 >= 2 a_0, where e_0=1 if endpoint=0 else 0.
=> E_0 >= 2 a_0 -1 - e_0.
For 1 <= i <= n-2:
E_{i-1} + E_i + e_i >= 2 a_i, where e_i=1 if endpoint=i else 0.
=> E_{i-1}+E_i >= 2 a_i - e_i.
For i=n-1:
E_{n-2} + e_{n-1} >= 2 a_{n-1}.
=> E_{n-2} >= 2 a_{n-1} - e_{n-1}.

Note a_i >=1 for x>0. For x=0, a_i=0 and min sum 0.

Parity: E_i parity = 1 if i < epos else 0. Thus for fixed epos, E_i must be at least some lower bound and have specified parity. We need minimize sum.

This is a chain of lower bounds on adjacent sums. Could solve for each endpoint with DP? Need O(n) per check, not O(n^2). But maybe there is a closed form / greedy.

Let's search memory of known solution. This problem might be from LeetCode Weekly? "Maximum Score of a Good Subarray"? Hmm. There is a known binary search + greedy: To check if min score x possible, compute required visits need[i] = (x + points[i] -1)//points[i]. Then simulate from left to right: maintain extra moves? There is a common greedy for "minimum moves to collect required visits on a line" where each time you are at i, if need[i] not satisfied, you bounce between i and i+1 until satisfied, then move right. I recall a LeetCode problem "Minimum Number of Moves to Make Array Complementary"? no. There is a problem "Collect Coins in a Tree" with "minimum time to visit nodes required times" maybe solution: For each i from 0 to n-2, if remaining visits at i > 0, do round trips between i and i+1; each round trip consumes 2 moves and gives one visit to i and i+1; then move to i+1. At the end, if last remaining visits >0, bounce with previous? Let's test.

Suppose we process left to right. Start at 0 after first move. We need visit counts. We can satisfy vertex i's remaining quota by bouncing between i and i+1 before moving to i+1. Each back-and-forth i->i+1->i adds 1 visit to i and 1 to i+1, costs 2 moves (internal). Then move to i+1 costs 1 move. At vertex i+1, it already got some visits from previous bounces (when bouncing at i). If still need more, bounce between i+1 and i+2, etc. At the end, for last vertex n-1, cannot bounce right, can bounce with n-2? But if we've moved to n-1, to add visits to n-1, we can bounce n-1 -> n-2 -> n-1, costing 2 per extra visit to n-1 and also adds to n-2. But maybe better to satisfy last vertex before final arrival by bouncing at n-2? Let's examine.

This greedy seems plausible: To satisfy quotas, for each i from 0 to n-2, before leaving i to i+1, ensure i has enough visits by doing round trips i<->i+1. Then move to i+1. This ensures all except last satisfied. At last, if need more, do round trips between n-1 and n-2. But doing round trips at last after arriving will also add visits to n-2, possibly over-satisfying, but okay. Is this always optimal? Need verify with examples and edge cases.

Let's formalize greedy for target x:
Let need[i] = ceil(x / points[i]) (visits required).
We start at 0 with one visit already? Actually first move to 0 counts. Let rem[i] = need[i].
At position i before moving to i+1, we have already accumulated some visits to i from previous bounces at i-1? Wait when bouncing at i-1 between i-1 and i, each round trip visits i once. So by the time we arrive at i (move from i-1 to i), i has received some visits. Also if we bounced at i-1, the final move from i-1 to i adds another visit to i. Need track.

Let's simulate with greedy:
Start at 0, visits[0]=1. For i=0:
If visits[0] < need[0], do k = need[0]-visits[0] round trips between 0 and1. Each round trip: move 0->1 (visit1), move1->0 (visit0). So visits[0]+=k, visits[1]+=k. Then move to 1: visits[1]+=1, position=1. Total moves: first move 1 + 2k + 1.
Now at i=1, visits[1] = previous visits from bounces at 0 + arrival. If < need[1], do k = need[1]-visits[1] round trips between 1 and2: each 1->2 (visit2), 2->1 (visit1). Then move to2. This seems.

At end i=n-1 after arrival, visits[n-1] may be less than need. To add visits to n-1, we can do round trips n-1 -> n-2 -> n-1. Each adds one visit to n-1 and one to n-2. Need k = need[n-1]-visits[n-1]. Cost 2k. End at n-1. Total moves = 1 + sum over i=0..n-2 (2*k_i + 1) + 2*k_last? Wait for i=0..n-2, after satisfying at i, move to i+1. For i=n-2, after satisfying n-2 by bouncing with n-1, move to n-1. Then satisfy last. So moves = 1 (initial) + Σ_{i=0}^{n-2} (2*k_i + 1) + 2*k_{n-1}, where k_i is extra round trips at i before moving right. But note k_i depends on visits accumulated from previous bounces. This is O(n).

So left-to-right greedy with no excursions beyond current frontier: For i=0..n-2, ensure vertex i satisfied by bouncing with i+1, then move to i+1. Let's define process more carefully with "frontier" and visits.

We process vertices 0..n-2:
At the start of processing vertex i, we are at position i (after moving from i-1, except i=0 after first move). All vertices <i are already satisfied. Vertices >i have not been visited yet? Actually vertex i+1 may have been visited during bounces at i? Not yet before processing i. Vertex i has some visits from previous bounces at i-1 and arrival. Specifically when processing i-1, bounces between i-1 and i visited i; then final move to i visited i. So visits[i] may be >0. Vertices >i have 0 visits. So need positive for them, so must continue.

At processing i:
- We are at i. Need visits[i] >= need[i]. If not, do k_i = need[i] - visits[i] round trips i->i+1->i. Each adds 1 to visits[i] and 1 to visits[i+1]. Cost 2k_i. After this, visits[i] satisfied.
- Then move to i+1: cost 1, visits[i+1] +=1.
This ensures i satisfied. Continue.

After processing n-2 and moving to n-1, we have visited n-1 (arrival) plus visits from bounces at n-2. If visits[n-1] < need[n-1], do k_{n-1} = need[n-1] - visits[n-1] round trips n-1 -> n-2 -> n-1. Each adds 1 to n-1 and 1 to n-2. Cost 2k. End at n-1. Total moves = 1 + Σ_{i=0}^{n-2} (2*k_i + 1) + 2*k_{n-1}. But if after processing n-2, before moving to n-1, visits[n-1] might already be >= need[n-1]? Then we don't need to move to n-1 at all? But need[n-1] positive; visits[n-1] from bounces at n-2 could satisfy it. If so, we can stop at n-2, saving the move to n-1 and also no need k_{n-1}. In the formula, if we always move to n-1, visits[n-1] gets +1, so it may be satisfied. But if it was already satisfied before the move, the move is unnecessary. To minimize, at i=n-2, after satisfying n-2 via bounces, if visits[n-1] >= need[n-1], we can stop at n-2 and not move. If not, we must move to n-1 (cost1) and maybe bounce. So the final move to n-1 is optional depending on whether n-1 already satisfied. More generally, what about at i<n-2, if visits[i+1] already >= need[i+1] and all later? Later >i+1 not visited, so cannot stop. Need move to i+1 to continue to later. So no saving.

Thus greedy minimal moves:
moves = 1 (initial to 0)
for i = 0 to n-2:
    if visits[i] < need[i]:
        k = need[i] - visits[i]
        moves += 2*k
        visits[i] += k
        visits[i+1] += k
    # Now at i. Need decide move to i+1.
    if i == n-2:
        # after satisfying n-2, check if n-1 already satisfied
        if visits[n-1] >= need[n-1]:
            # can stop at n-2, no move to n-1
            break/return moves
        else:
            moves += 1  # move to n-1
            visits[n-1] += 1
            # now satisfy last
            if visits[n-1] < need[n-1]:
                k = need[n-1] - visits[n-1]
                moves += 2*k
                visits[n-1] += k
                visits[n-2] += k
            return moves
    else:
        moves += 1
        visits[i+1] += 1
        # continue
But wait for i < n-2, after moving to i+1, visits[i+1] includes bounces from i plus arrival. Then next iteration will satisfy i+1 if needed. Good.

However, is this greedy optimal? Need prove or find counterexample. It resembles "minimum walk to satisfy vertex demands on a path with no reason to go back more than one edge before moving right" because any extra visits to a vertex can be done by bouncing with its right neighbor before moving on; going back left after moving right would add visits to left vertices already satisfied, possibly wasted but could help right? But need be careful: The greedy "satisfy current vertex by bouncing with right neighbor before moving right" may not be optimal if current vertex's remaining visits can be satisfied by bouncing with left neighbor after having moved right, while also helping right vertices? Let's test.

But there might be a case where satisfying current vertex by bouncing with left neighbor after moving right is cheaper because it doesn't require moving right then back? Let's use edge formula to verify greedy yields minimal sum E.

Let's derive greedy in terms of E_i. In greedy, for each i=0..n-2, we do k_i round trips across edge i (i<->i+1) before moving to i+1. Then one final crossing of edge i from i to i+1. Thus E_i = 2*k_i + 1 for i=0..n-2? Wait if we stop at n-2 without moving to n-1, then E_{n-2} = 2*k_{n-2} (no final crossing). If we move to n-1, E_{n-2} = 2*k_{n-2} + 1. Then at last, if need n-1 extra, do k_last round trips across edge n-2, adding 2*k_last. So E_{n-2} = 2*k_{n-2} + (1 if moved to n-1) + 2*k_last. For i < n-2, E_i = 2*k_i + 1 (odd). Thus greedy always crosses each edge i < n-2 odd number of times, meaning endpoint is to the right of all those cuts, i.e., endpoint >= n-2? Actually if E_i odd for all i< n-2, endpoint must be >= n-1? Wait endpoint epos: E_i odd iff i < epos. If E_i odd for i=0..n-3, then epos >= n-2. If E_{n-2} may be even or odd, endpoint could be n-2 or n-1. So greedy endpoint is either n-2 or n-1. It never ends left of n-2. But earlier example a=[100,1] n=2 greedy endpoint n-2=0 (since n=2, i=n-2=0, E0 even if stop at0). For n>2, greedy endpoint always n-1 or n-2? Let's check need=[100,1,1], n=3. Greedy:

Could optimal endpoint be left of n-2? To visit vertex n-1 (need positive), endpoint must be n-1 or n-2? If endpoint < n-1, to visit n-1 and return, edge n-2 even >0. Then endpoint could be < n-1. Example a=[100,1] n=2 endpoint0 (left of n-1). For n=3, could endpoint0 be optimal? Need visit vertex2 at least once, so must go to2 and return to0. That requires crossing edge0 and edge1 even times. Can there be a case where ending left saves moves because final vertex n-1 already satisfied and we don't need to move to n-1? Greedy already handles by stopping at n-2 if n-1 satisfied. Endpoint n-2. It never ends further left than n-2. Is it ever optimal to end at i < n-2? To satisfy vertices >i, you must have visited them and returned. Returning left across multiple edges costs extra moves compared to ending at or near the rightmost visited vertex. Since all vertices need positive, you must visit n-1. Ending at n-2 or n-1 seems natural; ending further left would require extra return traversals after last visit to right, which don't help satisfy any new vertex (all left already satisfied), so cannot reduce moves. Thus optimal endpoint is either n-2 or n-1? Wait if n-1 is satisfied before final move from n-2, endpoint n-2. If not, endpoint n-1. Could endpoint be n-3 if n-2 and n-1 satisfied earlier? To satisfy n-2 and n-1, you must have visited them; after last visit to n-1, returning to n-3 adds moves that don't help, so not optimal. So yes endpoint >= n-2. But what about if need n-1 is very low and need n-2 very high, maybe you satisfy n-1 during bounces at n-2 and stop at n-2. Endpoint n-2. Good.

However, is it always optimal to process left-to-right and never go back across an edge after moving past it? Greedy crosses each edge i < n-2 exactly 2*k_i+1 times: k_i round trips before final crossing. It never returns across edge i after moving to i+1. Could optimal require returning left across edge i after moving right, to satisfy vertex i (or earlier) later? But vertex i is satisfied before moving right; no need. Returning left would add visits to already satisfied vertices, wasted. Could it help satisfy vertex i+1? No, returning left doesn't visit i+1. Could it help satisfy some right vertex by enabling different route? No, path. So no.

But wait, what about satisfying vertex i by bouncing with i-1 after moving right instead of bouncing with i+1 before moving right? Suppose at vertex i, visits[i] insufficient. Greedy bounces i<->i+1, adding visits to i+1. Alternative: move to i+1, then bounce i+1<->i, adding visits to i and i+1, then move to i+1 again. This costs? Greedy: at i, if need d extra visits to i, do d round trips i->i+1->i (2d), then move to i+1 (1). Total 2d+1, visits i +d, i+1 +d, then arrival +1. Alternative: move to i+1 (1), then do d round trips i+1->i->i+1 (2d), ending at i+1. Total 1+2d, visits i +d, i+1 +d, and no extra arrival? Actually after round trips ending at i+1, you are at i+1; visits i+1 got d from round trips plus initial arrival? Let's count: start at i. Move to i+1: visit i+1 +1. Round trip i+1->i->i+1: visit i +1, i+1 +1. Repeat d times: i+d, i+1+d. Total visits i+d, i+1 + (1+d). Cost 1+2d. Greedy cost 2d+1, visits i+d, i+1 + d +1 (final arrival). Same cost and visits. So equivalent! Greedy's round trip direction doesn't matter. Good.

What about satisfying vertex i by bouncing with i-1 after having moved to i+1? That would require returning left across edge i-1, adding visits to i-1 (satisfied) and i, then go right again. Cost extra compared to bouncing with i+1? Let's compare: Need d extra visits to i. Bouncing with i+1 costs 2d and also visits i+1. Bouncing with i-1 after moving to i+1: from i+1, move to i (cost1, visit i), move to i-1 (cost1, visit i-1), move to i (cost1, visit i), ... to get d visits to i, each visit to i from i-1 requires being at i-1; round trip i->i-1->i costs 2 per visit, plus need get from i+1 to i first and eventually back to i+1? More expensive and doesn't help right. So no.

Thus greedy likely optimal.

But let's verify with edge formula and derive closed-form for greedy to ensure O(n) check and no overflow.

Let's define need[i] = ceil(K / points[i]) = (K + points[i] - 1)//points[i].

Greedy simulation with visits array? n up to 5e4, binary search ~60? Actually K up to max? m up to1e9, points up to1e6, max min maybe m*max points? Wait each move adds points[i] to one game. We need simulate greedy.

Pseudo:
def can(K):
    if K == 0: return True
    moves = 1  # first move to index 0
    # visits to current vertex 0 after first move
    cur = 1  # visits[0]
    # We'll process i from 0 to n-2
    for i in range(n-1):
        need_cur = (K + points[i] -1)//points[i]
        # At this point we are at vertex i, cur = visits[i] so far.
        if cur < need_cur:
            k = need_cur - cur
            moves += 2 * k
            if moves > m: return False  # can early exit
            cur = need_cur
            # visits to i+1 increase by k
            next_visits = k
        else:
            next_visits = 0
        # Now decide move to i+1
        if i == n-2:
            # last edge, vertex n-1
            need_next = (K + points[i+1] -1)//points[i+1]
            # visits to n-1 currently = next_visits (from bounces)
            if next_visits >= need_next:
                # can stop at i, no move to n-1
                return moves <= m
            else:
                # move to n-1
                moves += 1
                if moves > m: return False
                next_visits += 1
                # now at n-1, need maybe bounce with n-2
                if next_visits < need_next:
                    k = need_next - next_visits
                    moves += 2 * k
                    if moves > m: return False
                    # visits[n-2] += k (not needed)
                return moves <= m
        else:
            # move to i+1
            moves += 1
            if moves > m: return False
            # arrival adds one visit to i+1
            cur = next_visits + 1
            # continue to next i (i+1)
    # If n==? n>=2, loop executes. For n=1 not allowed. But handle.
    return moves <= m

Wait, for i < n-2, after moving to i+1, cur = visits[i+1] = next_visits + 1. But next_visits includes visits from bounces at i. Good. But what about visits to i+1 from earlier bounces at i-1? Those were counted in cur when we arrived at i? Let's trace:
At start i=0, cur=1 (visit0). next_visits from bounces at0 = k0. Move to1, cur = k0 +1. This includes bounces at0 plus arrival. Good.
At i=1, if cur<need1, k1 bounces 1<->2. next_visits for vertex2 = k1 (from bounces at1). But vertex2 may have been visited earlier? No, before processing i=1, vertex2 hasn't been visited (unless i=0 bounces only to1). So next_visits = k1. Move to2, cur = k1+1. Good.
At i=2, cur includes bounces at1 and arrival. Good.

But wait, when processing i=1, if we did k1 bounces, visits to vertex2 increase by k1. Then move to2, arrival +1. So cur for i=2 = k1+1. Good.

Need ensure if cur < need_cur, k = need_cur - cur. But cur might be huge; moves early exit.

Now, is the final handling correct for n=2? Loop i=0 only (n-1=1). cur=1. If need0>1, bounce with vertex1: next_visits=k. i==n-2. need_next = need1. If next_visits >= need1, stop at0, moves =1+2k. This matches example need0=2, need1=1: k=1, next_visits=1 >=1, moves=3. Good.
If need0=1, need1=100: cur=1 satisfied, next_visits=0. i==n-2, need_next=100, next_visits<need, move to1: moves=2, next_visits=1, k=99, moves=200. Matches earlier. Good.

But wait, for n=2 and need0=1, need1=1: moves? Greedy: i=0 cur=1 satisfied, next_visits=0, need_next=1, move to1 moves=2, next_visits=1 satisfied, return moves=2. Is minimal? Need visit both vertices once: moves 2 (0 then1). Yes.

Now, is there any case where at i==n-2, after satisfying n-2, next_visits >= need_next, we stop at n-2. But what if n-2's need required bounces, and those bounces visited n-1 enough. Good. If n-2's need was already satisfied without bounces, next_visits=0. Since need_next positive, next_visits>=need_next false, so move to n-1. Good.

Now, is greedy with left-to-right and endpoint n-2/n-1 always optimal? Let's try to prove or find counterexample using edge formula.

Let's attempt to compare greedy to minimal edge sum. Greedy yields E_i = 2*k_i + 1 for i=0..n-3. For edge n-2, E_{n-2} = 2*k_{n-2} + (0 or 1) + 2*k_last. The k_i are determined by need and visits accumulated. Could there be a solution with some E_i even for i<n-2 (meaning returned left after moving past) that has smaller sum? Returning left across edge i adds at least 2 traversals (one right, one left) and may add visits to vertices on both sides. But those visits could reduce need for bounces elsewhere. Need ensure no net benefit.

Consider a scenario where need at vertex i is low, but need at i+1 is high. Greedy moves to i+1 and bounces i+1<->i+2. This crosses edge i only once (to arrive). It doesn't help i+1 by crossing edge i back and forth. Alternative: after arriving at i+1, bounce i+1<->i to add visits to i+1. This crosses edge i extra times and adds visits to i (already satisfied) and i+1. Could this reduce total moves compared to bouncing i+1<->i+2? Bouncing with left neighbor costs same 2 per visit to i+1, but also visits i (wasted) instead of i+2 (which may need visits). If i+2 needs visits, bouncing with right is better because it helps i+2. If i+2 already satisfied, maybe bouncing with left could be used to satisfy i+1 without over-satisfying i+2? But over-satisfying i+2 is not harmful; and if i+2 already satisfied, extra visits don't hurt. Cost same 2 per visit to i+1. However, bouncing with left requires being at i+1 and moving to i; after each bounce you return to i+1. It crosses edge i twice per visit. Bouncing with right crosses edge i+1 twice per visit. Both cost 2. If edge i+1 doesn't exist (last vertex), must bounce left. Greedy does that at last. For internal i+1, if i+2 need already satisfied, greedy may still bounce right if i+1 need not satisfied? Wait greedy at vertex i+1 bounces with i+2 before moving to i+2. If i+2 need is already satisfied, greedy might stop at i+1 (if i+1 is n-2) or if not last, it still needs to move to i+2 to visit later vertices, so bouncing right helps i+2. If all later vertices already satisfied? Only possible if i+1 is n-2? Because later vertices beyond i+2 not visited yet. So for i+1 < n-2, later vertices need visits, so bouncing right helps i+2, which is good. Thus right bounce is at least as good.

What about using left bounce to satisfy i+1 while also satisfying i? i already satisfied, no need. So no advantage.

Could there be advantage to satisfy vertex i by bouncing with i-1 after moving right, because bouncing with i+1 would add too many visits to i+1 causing need to be exceeded and maybe requiring fewer? Extra visits never hurt; but they might change k for i+1: if i+1 gets more visits, k_{i+1} decreases, which is good. So right bounce is beneficial.

Thus greedy seems optimal.

But let's be rigorous: We can define a canonical optimal walk with no "unnecessary" left moves across an edge after the last time moving right across it. For each edge i, consider the last time the walk moves from i to i+1 (rightward). After that, it never crosses edge i again (otherwise would be a later rightward crossing). Thus all traversals of edge i before that last rightward crossing consist of some number of round trips plus maybe? Starting at 0, to first cross edge i rightward, must have crossed edges <i. The walk can be decomposed into phases: For each i, before the final right crossing of edge i, it may bounce across edge i multiple times. Each time it crosses from i to i+1 and back to i (except final) adds one visit to i and i+1. Greedy does exactly the minimum number of such bounces needed to satisfy vertex i before final crossing, because after final crossing it never returns to i, so vertex i cannot be visited later. Wait is it true after final crossing of edge i, vertex i cannot be visited later? If final crossing is from i to i+1, after that no more crossing edge i, so cannot be at i. Thus all visits to vertex i must occur before or at the final crossing? The final crossing leaves i, so the last visit to i is just before final crossing. Therefore vertex i's quota must be satisfied before final crossing. Greedy ensures that. Moreover, visits to i before final crossing can only come from:
- initial start if i=0,
- crossings from i-1 to i (arrivals) and bounces across edge i (i+1->i arrivals).
Greedy accounts for arrivals from left and bounces with right. It does not include bounces with left after arriving from left? Wait before final crossing of edge i, the walk may have crossed edge i-1 multiple times. It could arrive at i from i-1, then bounce back to i-1 and later arrive again. Those arrivals from left also count as visits to i. Greedy's cur when at i includes exactly one arrival from i-1 (the final crossing of edge i-1) plus visits from bounces at i-1? Let's see: In greedy, before processing i, we have just crossed edge i-1 from i-1 to i for the last time? Actually for i>0, the move from i-1 to i is the final crossing of edge i-1 (since greedy never returns). So visits to i before final crossing of edge i include:
- visits during bounces at i-1 across edge i-1 (which landed at i and returned to i-1) — these occurred before the final crossing of edge i-1, and greedy counted them in next_visits from previous step.
- the final arrival from i-1 to i — counted as +1.
- bounces across edge i (i<->i+1) — counted as k_i.
No other visits. So greedy accounts for all possible visits to i before final crossing of edge i. In an arbitrary optimal walk, before final crossing of edge i, there could be multiple arrivals from i-1 (if edge i-1 crossed multiple times), not just one. Those extra arrivals would add visits to i but also require extra traversals of edge i-1. Could they reduce need for bounces across edge i and thus total moves? Greedy assumes exactly one final arrival from left. Maybe extra arrivals from left could be beneficial? Let's test.

Example: need = [1, 100, 1]? Greedy had one arrival to1, then bounces with2. Extra arrivals from0 to1 would require bouncing 0<->1, adding visits to0 (wasted) and1, cost2 per extra visit to1. Bouncing 1<->2 also cost2 per visit to1 and helps2. If need2 low, both cost same; if need2 high, right bounce better. So extra left arrivals not better.

But what if edge i+1 doesn't exist? For last vertex n-1, after final crossing edge n-2, no right neighbor; extra visits to n-1 can only be obtained by bounces across edge n-2 (left neighbor). Greedy does that. Extra arrivals from left before final crossing? For vertex n-1, before final crossing, you can't be at n-1 except by crossing edge n-2. Each visit to n-1 requires a rightward crossing of edge n-2. Greedy counts visits from bounces at n-2 (which cross to n-1 and back) plus final arrival. Extra arrivals from left before final crossing are exactly those bounces. Good.

So greedy is essentially: For each edge i, before its final rightward crossing, use the minimum number of round trips across edge i to satisfy vertex i, given visits already accumulated from previous edge's final crossing and bounces. This is optimal because any additional visits to vertex i before final crossing must involve crossing edge i (right neighbor) or edge i-1 (left neighbor). Crossings of edge i-1 after the final crossing of edge i-1? Wait before final crossing of edge i, the final crossing of edge i-1 may not have happened? Actually to be at i, you must have crossed edge i-1 at least once. The last time you cross edge i-1 before final crossing of edge i could be the arrival. Extra arrivals from i-1 before that last arrival would require crossing edge i-1 back and forth, which are traversals of edge i-1 before its final crossing. Those could be used to satisfy vertex i-1 or i. Greedy uses bounces across edge i-1 to satisfy vertex i-1 before final crossing of edge i-1; those bounces also visit i. So extra arrivals from left are already considered as bounces at i-1. They are not "extra" beyond greedy; they are exactly k_{i-1}. If vertex i still needs more after those, greedy uses bounces across edge i. Could it be better to do more bounces at i-1 instead of at i to satisfy i? Cost per visit to i is 2 in both cases. Bounces at i-1 add visits to i-1 (already satisfied) and i; bounces at i add visits to i and i+1. If i+1 needs visits, bounces at i are better or equal. If i+1 doesn't need visits (maybe because it's beyond final endpoint? But need positive for all vertices, so eventually i+1 needs visits if i+1 <= n-1. However, if i+1 is already satisfied by previous bounces? For i+1, before final crossing of edge i, it may have been visited during bounces at i? Wait bounces at i cross to i+1 and back, so they visit i+1. If we choose bounces at i-1 instead, i+1 gets fewer visits, so later need more bounces at i+1 or crossing. Thus right bounce is at least as good. So greedy minimal.

Let's try to find a counterexample with high need at i+1 and low need at i+2: Greedy bounces at i to satisfy i, giving i+1 many visits, reducing later bounces. Good.

What about high need at i-1? Greedy satisfied i-1 earlier. Extra bounces at i-1 to satisfy i would over-satisfy i-1, no benefit.

Thus okay.

Now, can we simplify check to avoid storing visits? The above is okay. But let's verify early exit with moves > m doesn't break when moves could later not increase? Moves only increases, so safe. But there is subtlety: We are allowed "at most m moves", not exactly. Greedy computes minimal moves for that strategy. If minimal moves > m, infeasible. If minimal moves <=m, feasible. Early exit when moves > m okay.

Consider any walk satisfying quotas. Let R_i be the last time the walk moves from i to i+1 (rightward) for each edge i=0..n-2. Since all vertices need positive, such a move exists for every i? To visit vertex n-1, must cross every edge rightward at least once, so yes for all i. Let t_i be the step (move number) of the last rightward crossing of edge i. Because to cross edge i+1 rightward, you must have crossed edge i rightward earlier; but the last rightward crossing of edge i could occur after last rightward crossing of edge i+1? Actually if you cross edge i+1 rightward, you are at i+1; to later cross edge i rightward again, you must have returned left across edge i+1? Wait last rightward crossing of edge i means moving from i to i+1. After that, you never move from i to i+1 again, but you could be at i+1 and later move left to i? That would be a leftward crossing of edge i after the last rightward crossing. Then you would be at i, and to get back to i+1 you'd need another rightward crossing, contradicting last. So after last rightward crossing of edge i, you never cross edge i again. Thus you remain on the right side of edge i forever. Therefore, the last rightward crossing of edge i occurs after the last rightward crossing of edge i-1? To be at i for that crossing, you must have crossed edge i-1 rightward and not returned left after? Actually you could have crossed edge i-1 rightward, then left, then right multiple times; the last rightward crossing of edge i-1 must occur before or at the time you are at i for the last rightward crossing of edge i? If after last rightward crossing of edge i you never cross edge i, you stay in suffix i+1..n-1. Thus you cannot cross edge i-1 rightward after that? Wait edge i-1 is to the left of i. If after last rightward crossing of edge i you are at i+1 or right, you are also to the right of edge i-1. You could later cross edge i-1 rightward? To cross edge i-1 rightward, you need be at i-1, which would require crossing edge i leftward, impossible. So no. Thus last rightward crossings occur in increasing order: t_0 < t_1 < ... < t_{n-2}. Good.

For each edge i, between the last rightward crossing of edge i-1 (or start for i=0) and the last rightward crossing of edge i, the walk is confined to vertices i and i+1? Greedy does not do excursions beyond i+1 before final crossing of edge i; it satisfies i by bouncing with i+1 only, then crosses to i+1 and never returns. Now, let's think of a more complex case where greedy's k_i are not minimal because visits to current vertex could come from "future" excursions to right and back, which also satisfy future vertices. Wait if you visit current vertex i after final crossing of edge i, impossible because final crossing of edge i is last time leaving i to right; to visit i later you'd have to cross edge i leftward, which is allowed after final rightward crossing? Let's revisit definition: "last rightward crossing" of edge i is last move from i to i+1. After that, you could later move from i+1 to i (leftward), visiting i. But then to continue to right, you'd need another rightward crossing, contradiction. However, if the walk ends at i after that leftward crossing, then there is no later rightward crossing. So the "last rightward crossing" is not necessarily the last time at i; the walk can end at i after returning left. In that case, after last rightward crossing of edge i, there is a leftward crossing, and no more rightward. So vertex i can be visited after the last rightward crossing, as the endpoint. This is exactly the case endpoint i (or left of i? Actually if you cross edge i leftward and end at i, endpoint i). Greedy's endpoint is n-2 or n-1, so for edges before endpoint, it never returns left after last rightward crossing. But an optimal walk might end at i < n-2, meaning for edge i, after last rightward crossing, it returns left and ends at i. That would add visits to vertex i after it was "satisfied" before crossing, potentially allowing fewer visits before crossing? Wait if you know you will end at i, you could satisfy some of vertex i's quota after the last rightward crossing instead of before, by returning left at the end. This might reduce bounces before crossing and also allow ending left, maybe saving moves? Earlier I dismissed ending left as extra return moves, but maybe it can replace bounces that would otherwise be needed to satisfy vertex i before crossing, and also avoid moving to later? So greedy candidates n-2/n-1 seem optimal.

Let's try to prove via "last rightward crossing" and no need to return left beyond n-2. Since all vertices need positive, the walk must visit n-1. Consider the last visit to n-1. After that, the walk may move left and end at some vertex e <= n-1. If e < n-2, then after leaving n-1, it must traverse edges n-2, n-3, ..., e+1 leftward. These traversals add visits to vertices on the left, but all vertices already have at least their required visits (since they were visited before reaching n-1; but maybe some vertex's quota was not yet satisfied and these leftward moves help satisfy it). Could ending left help satisfy quotas of left vertices that were not satisfied before reaching n-1? In greedy, all left vertices are satisfied before moving right. But an optimal walk might delay satisfying some left vertex until after visiting n-1, then satisfy it while returning left, potentially reducing bounces before moving right. Is that ever beneficial? Let's examine.

Example need [100,1,1] n=3. Greedy satisfied vertex0 before moving right with 99 bounces. Alternative: go to n-1 first, then return to0, satisfying vertex0 on return? Sequence 0,1,2,1,0 gives c0=2 only, not100. To get c0=100, need many returns to0, which would require bounces between0 and1 anyway. Could combine with visits to n-1? Maybe sequence 0,1,2,1,0,1,0,1,0...? Need c0=100, c2=1. Minimal? Greedy 201? Wait need [100,1,1] greedy total201? Earlier for [100,1,1] n=3: i0 k99 moves199, move1 moves200, move2 moves201. Total201. Alternative: 0,1,2,1,0,1,0,1,0... To get c0=100 and c2=1, after first visit to2, bounce 0<->1. Sequence: 0,1,2,1,0 (c0=2,c1=2,c2=1), then need 98 more c0: each bounce 0->1->0 adds c0 and c1, cost2. Total first 5 +196 =201. Same. So delaying doesn't improve.

What about need [100,1,100]? Greedy 399. Alternative: visit2 early then bounce0<->1 and2<->1? Need both ends high. Minimal edge sum 398. Greedy achieves.

Could there be a case where delaying satisfaction of a left vertex until after visiting right reduces total because the return path provides some visits for free? In a path, to return left, each move left visits a vertex. Those visits count. Greedy's left-to-right path also visits each vertex once for free. Extra left moves after reaching right are not free; they cost moves and add visits. They could replace bounces that would otherwise be done before moving right. But bounces before moving right also cost moves and add visits to right neighbor. The cost per extra visit to a left vertex is 2 (go left and return right) if you need to end right, or 1 if it's the final move to end left. If you end left, the final leftward traversal gives one visit to each vertex along the way at cost 1 per vertex, which is cheaper than bounces. Could this make ending left optimal? Let's test.

Suppose need high at vertex0, low at others. Greedy bounces 0<->1 to satisfy0, then moves right and ends at n-1 or n-2. Alternative: go right to n-1, then return left to0, satisfying vertex0 on the final return with one visit per move, but you need many visits to0; returning left only gives one visit to0 at the very end. To get high c0, you need multiple returns to0, each requires going right again and left, costing 2 per extra visit. Same as bounces. If you end at0, the last return gives one visit "free" compared to a round trip? Let's compare n=2 need a=100,b=1. Greedy endpoint0: 99 bounces 0<->1, end0, moves199. It uses final return? Actually each bounce includes return to0; the last bounce ends at0, so all 99 extra visits to0 cost 2 each. Alternative: go to1, then return to0, then bounce? To get 100 visits to0, need 99 returns to0. Each return from1 to0 costs1, but to return again need go to1 cost1, so pair cost2. Last return cost1 if end0. Greedy's last bounce: 0->1 (cost1), 1->0 (cost1), total2 for last extra visit. But if instead after 98 bounces, you are at0 with c0=99, c1=98. Need one more c0 and c1>=1. You could move 0->1 (c1=99), then 1->0 (c0=100) cost2. Same. No free lunch because to get a visit to0 you must be at1 and move to0; being at1 requires a move from0 to1 unless you were already at1. If you end at0, the last move from1 to0 gives c0, but you had to get to1, which cost1. So total2. Thus no cheaper.

What about satisfying vertex i by returning left after visiting right, where the return path passes through i and also intermediate vertices. It gives one visit to each intermediate vertex at cost1 per move, which is cheaper than bouncing to satisfy each individually? But to get back to right (if not ending), you'd need go right again, cost. If ending left, the entire return path from n-1 to e gives one visit to each vertex on the path at cost1 per vertex. Greedy's initial left-to-right path also gives one visit to each vertex at cost1 per vertex. Extra visits require extra traversals. So no advantage.

I think greedy is correct.

Let's derive a compact check from greedy.

Let need_i. We can simulate with `cur` visits to current vertex. But maybe there is a recurrence:
At vertex i, before moving right, `cur` = visits[i] accumulated. If cur < need_i, we need k = need_i - cur bounces across edge i. Each bounce costs 2 and adds 1 to i+1. Then move right costs 1 and adds 1 to i+1. So after moving to i+1, visits[i+1] = k + 1 (from bounces + arrival). But wait if i+1 had previous visits from earlier? For i+1, before processing i, it had 0 visits (since not reached). So yes. Thus we can compute next_cur = k + 1. This is simple.

For i from 0 to n-2:
    cur = visits[i] (initially 1 for i=0; for i>0, computed as k_{i-1}+1)
    if cur < need_i:
        k = need_i - cur
        moves += 2*k
        cur = need_i
    else:
        k = 0
    # Now if i < n-2, must move to i+1:
    if i < n-2:
        moves += 1
        cur_next = k + 1  # because bounces at i visited i+1 k times, then arrival
    else:
        # i == n-2, last edge
        # visits to n-1 currently = k (from bounces at n-2)
        # need_next = need_{n-1}
        if k >= need_next:
            # stop at n-2
            return moves <= m
        else:
            moves += 1  # move to n-1
            # visits to n-1 = k+1
            if k+1 < need_next:
                moves += 2 * (need_next - (k+1))
            return moves <= m

Wait, for i < n-2, cur_next = k + 1. But what about visits to i+1 from bounces at i-1? Those were included in cur at i? Let's trace: At i=1, cur = k0 +1. If cur < need1, k1 = need1 - cur. Bounces at i=1 add k1 visits to i+1=2. Then move to2, cur_next = k1 +1. Good. So recurrence cur_next = k_i + 1, where k_i is bounces at current vertex i. This is independent of cur except k_i depends on cur.

But wait, if cur > need_i, we set k=0. Then cur_next = 1. But visits to i+1 from bounces at i-1? For i+1, no previous visits except maybe if i+1 was visited during bounces at i-1? Actually bounces at i-1 are between i-1 and i, not i+1. So no. Good.

Let's test with need [2,1,1]:

Let's write check with this recurrence.

Pseudo:
def can(x):
    if x == 0: return True
    moves = 1  # first move to 0
    cur = 1    # visits to current vertex (0)
    for i in range(n-1):
        need = (x + points[i] - 1)//points[i]
        if cur < need:
            k = need - cur
            moves += 2*k
            if moves > m: return False
            cur = need
        else:
            k = 0
        if i == n-2:
            need_next = (x + points[n-1] - 1)//points[n-1]
            # visits to last vertex so far = k
            if k >= need_next:
                return moves <= m
            moves += 1
            if moves > m: return False
            # now visits to last = k+1
            if k + 1 < need_next:
                moves += 2 * (need_next - k - 1)
                if moves > m: return False
            return True
        else:
            moves += 1
            if moves > m: return False
            cur = k + 1
    # n>=2, loop always returns at i=n-2. But if n=1? not.
    return moves <= m

Wait, for i < n-2, after moving to i+1, cur = k + 1. But what if k=0 and cur_next=1; yes. However, if cur > need, k=0, but there may have been bounces at i? No, if cur already satisfied, no bounces. But visits to i+1 from previous bounces? None. Good.

But hold on: In the earlier simulation, when at vertex i, `cur` could be greater than need due to previous bounces. We set k=0. But those previous bounces at i-1 also added visits to i, and then arrival added one. That's cur. For vertex i+1, it has not been visited yet, except if i+1 was the right neighbor in bounces at i? Not yet. So cur_next = 1 if no bounces at i. Correct.

Now, is the final last vertex handling correct when k from bounces at n-2 already satisfies need_next? We stop at n-2. But what if n-2's need was satisfied without bounces (k=0) and need_next=0? For x>0 need_next>=1, so move. For x=0 handled.

Let's test with need [100,1,1,100] using recurrence:
n=4.
i=0: cur1 need100 k99 moves199; i<2 move moves200 cur=100.
i=1: cur100 need1 k0; i<2 move moves201 cur=1.
i=2 (n-2): cur1 need1 k0; need_next100, k=0<100, moves202, k+1=1, extra99 moves400. Total400. Good.

Need [100,1,100,1]:
i=0 k99 moves199 move to1 moves200 cur100.
i=1 cur100 need1 k0 move to2 moves201 cur1.
i=2 cur1 need100 k99 moves399; i==n-2, need_next1, k=99>=1 stop. Total399. Good.

Now, let's compare recurrence to edge formula for arbitrary endpoint n-2/n-1. It seems good.

But wait, what about if at vertex i, cur < need, we do k bounces. Each bounce i->i+1->i adds one visit to i and one to i+1. But if i+1 is the last vertex and i == n-2, then after k bounces, visits to last = k. If k >= need_next, stop at i. If not, move to last (+1) and maybe bounce last->i->last. In recurrence, if k+1 < need_next, extra = need_next - (k+1), moves +=2*extra. But those extra bounces also add visits to i (n-2), which is already satisfied. Fine. Could there be a better way to satisfy last vertex before moving to it by doing more bounces at n-2? That's exactly k? Wait k was determined by need of n-2. If need_next > k, we might choose to do more bounces at n-2 than needed for n-2 to satisfy last vertex, then stop at n-2 without moving to last. Let's examine! This is important.

At i = n-2, we are at n-2. We need to satisfy n-2 and n-1. Greedy first satisfies n-2 by doing k = need[n-2] - cur bounces. This gives k visits to n-1. If k < need[n-1], it then moves to n-1 and maybe bounces. But alternative: do additional bounces between n-2 and n-1 before moving to n-1, each adds one visit to n-1 and one to n-2 (over-satisfying n-2). Then stop at n-2 if n-1 satisfied, avoiding the move to n-1. Could this be cheaper than moving to n-1 and bouncing? Let's compare.

Suppose after satisfying n-2, we have k visits to n-1, need_next = b > k. Option A (greedy): move to n-1 (cost1), then if needed do (b - (k+1)) round trips n-1->n-2->n-1 (cost2 each). Total extra cost = 1 + 2*(b - k -1) = 2b - 2k -1. End at n-1. Option B: do extra round trips n-2->n-1->n-2 before moving. Each costs2 and adds one visit to n-1. Need b - k extra round trips. Then stop at n-2. Total extra cost = 2*(b - k). Compare Option A vs B: A cost = 2b -2k -1, B cost = 2b -2k. A is 1 less! So moving to n-1 and bouncing is cheaper by 1, because the move to n-1 itself provides a free visit to n-1, reducing needed bounces. Thus greedy's option A is better. What if b = k+1? A cost =1 (just move), B cost=2 (one extra bounce). A better. What if b <= k? Greedy stops at n-2 cost0; B would need no extra, also stop at n-2. So greedy optimal. Good.

Similarly, for i < n-2, could we do extra bounces at i to satisfy i+1 before moving, reducing later moves? Suppose at vertex i, after satisfying i with k bounces, visits to i+1 = k. If need[i+1] > k, we might do extra bounces i<->i+1 to give i+1 more visits, then move to i+1. Each extra bounce costs2 and adds one visit to i+1. Alternatively, move to i+1 (cost1, adds one visit), then later satisfy i+1 by bouncing with i+2 (cost2 per visit). Which is cheaper? Extra bounce before moving: cost2 for one visit to i+1. Move first: the move itself gives one visit to i+1 at cost1, and if more needed, bounces with i+2 cost2 per visit. So moving first is always at least as good because the move gives a "free" visit compared to a bounce. Therefore greedy should not do extra bounces at i to satisfy i+1 before moving; it should move and then bounce with right neighbor. This is key. Our recurrence does that. Good.

What about satisfying i+1 by bouncing with i (left) after moving to i+1 instead of with i+2? If i+1 is not last, bouncing right helps i+2; if i+2 needs visits, right is better. If i+2 doesn't need visits (but all need positive, so it does eventually), right is still not worse. The move to i+1 gives one visit; then if need i+1 still not met, bouncing with i+2 costs2 per visit and helps i+2. Bouncing with i would cost2 per visit and helps i (already satisfied). So right is better. Thus greedy.

Now, can we further simplify recurrence to avoid `cur` variable? We can compute `cur` as previous k + 1. But need current need. Let's write:

        moves += 1
        if k + 1 < need_last:

        return moves <= m
    else:
        moves += 1
        cur = k + 1

But wait, `cur` after satisfying current is set to need (if cur<need) or remains cur. Then for next iteration, we set cur = k + 1, not `cur`? Let's verify: At vertex i, after bounces, visits to i+1 = k (from bounces). Then move to i+1 adds 1. So visits[i+1] = k + 1. This is independent of `cur` (except k). So yes, for next iteration cur = k + 1. We don't need to carry over anything else. But is it possible that vertex i+1 was visited before processing i due to bounces at i-1? No, bounces at i-1 are between i-1 and i, not i+1. What about if i+1 was visited as part of bounces at i-2? No, path. So no.

Let's write can carefully:

def can(x):
    if x == 0:
        return True
    moves = 1
    cur = 1
    for i in range(n - 1):
        need = (x + points[i] - 1) // points[i]
        if cur < need:
            k = need - cur
            moves += 2 * k
            if moves > m:
                return False
            cur = need
        else:
            k = 0
        if i == n - 2:
            need_last = (x + points[-1] - 1) // points[-1]
            if k >= need_last:
                return moves <= m
            moves += 1
            if moves > m:
                return False
            if k + 1 < need_last:
                moves += 2 * (need_last - k - 1)
                if moves > m:
                    return False
            return True
        else:
            moves += 1
            if moves > m:
                return False
            cur = k + 1
    return moves <= m  # unreachable for n>=2

Wait, when cur < need, we set cur = need. But if cur > need, cur remains cur. However, for next iteration we overwrite cur = k+1. So setting cur=need not actually needed except maybe for clarity. But if i==n-2, we don't use cur after. So can omit. But keep.

```
class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, points: List[int], m: int) -> int:
        n = len(points)
        max_p = max(points)
        
        def can(x: int) -> bool:
            if x == 0:
                return True
            moves = 1
            cur = 1
            last_need = (x + points[-1] - 1) // points[-1]
            for i in range(n - 1):
                need = (x + points[i] - 1) // points[i]
                if cur < need:
                    k = need - cur
                    moves += 2 * k
                    if moves > m:
                        return False
                else:
                    k = 0
                if i == n - 2:
                    if k >= last_need:
                        return moves <= m
                    moves += 1
                    if moves > m:
                        return False
                    if k + 1 < last_need:
                        moves += 2 * (last_need - k - 1)
                        if moves > m:
                            return False
                    return True
                else:
                    moves += 1
                    if moves > m:
                        return False
                    cur = k + 1
            return moves <= m
```

Let's try to construct a case where doing extra bounces at current vertex to satisfy next vertex before moving is cheaper because moving first and bouncing with right neighbor might require crossing an extra edge? Wait moving first from i to i+1 crosses edge i (cost1) and gives a visit to i+1. Then bouncing with i+2 crosses edge i+1 twice per extra visit. Extra bounces at i before moving cross edge i twice per extra visit to i+1. Which is cheaper? Extra bounce before moving: cost2 per visit to i+1, but also does not require moving to i+1? Actually after extra bounce, you are at i, then must move to i+1 cost1. So for one extra visit to i+1 before moving, total extra cost = 2 (bounce) + 1 (move) =3, and you get one visit to i+1 plus the eventual arrival? Wait if you do one extra bounce i->i+1->i, you visit i+1 once, then move i->i+1, visit i+1 second time. Total cost 3, visits to i+1 +2. If instead move first (cost1, visit i+1), then do one bounce i+1->i+2->i+1 (cost2, visit i+1), total cost3, visits to i+1 +2. Same cost! So extra bounce before moving is not more expensive; it's equivalent in cost per visit to i+1? Let's compare carefully.

At vertex i, after satisfying i, suppose we want to add one extra visit to i+1 before moving. Option A (extra bounce at i): move i->i+1 (visit i+1), move i+1->i, then move i->i+1 (visit i+1). Cost3, gives 2 visits to i+1? Actually initial state at i. The extra bounce includes first move to i+1 (visit), return to i, then final move to i+1 (visit). So yes 2 visits to i+1, cost3. Option B (move first then bounce with right): move i->i+1 (visit), then bounce i+1->i+2->i+1 (visit i+1), cost3, gives 2 visits to i+1. Same cost. But Option A also over-satisfies i? The return to i adds a visit to i (already satisfied), while Option B adds a visit to i+2. If i+2 needs visits, Option B is better. If i+2 doesn't need visits (but all need positive, eventually it does), Option B helps. So moving first is at least as good. If i+1 is last vertex, Option B would bounce with i (left) after moving, cost? Move to last (1), bounce last->i->last (2) =3 for one extra visit to last after arrival? Let's compare to extra bounce before moving at n-2: bounce n-2->last->n-2 (2), then move to last (1) =3, gives 2 visits to last. Same. So no difference. Greedy chooses move first, which is fine.

But greedy doesn't do excursions. Could an excursion to i+2 before satisfying i+1 reduce cost? Suppose at i, instead of moving to i+1 and bouncing with i+2, you could go to i+2, back to i+1, etc. This is equivalent to moving first and bouncing with right, but also includes extra traversals of edge i? Let's see: From i, to visit i+2 and return to i+1, sequence i->i+1->i+2->i+1. Cost3, visits i+1 twice, i+2 once. This is exactly Option B (move to i+1 then bounce i+1->i+2->i+1) cost3. Let's attempt to prove recurrence by induction for my own confidence:

Define a canonical walk that never moves left from i+1 to i after the first time it moves from i to i+1? Actually greedy's walk: for each i, it crosses edge i rightward exactly once (except maybe edge n-2 crossed once or zero? Actually for i<n-2, crosses right once; for n-2, crosses right once if last not satisfied before, else zero). It never crosses any edge leftward after moving past it. This is a "sweep" with local bounces before crossing each edge. We need show any optimal walk can be transformed to this form without increasing moves.

Take any optimal walk. Consider the first time it reaches vertex n-1? Or consider the last time it crosses each edge rightward. Let t_i be last rightward crossing of edge i. As argued, t_0 < t_1 < ... < t_{n-2}. For each i, before t_i, the walk may cross edge i multiple times. The visits to vertex i before t_i must be at least need_i, because after t_i, can the walk visit i again? It could cross edge i leftward after t_i and end left of i, as discussed. If it does, then t_i is not the last crossing of edge i, only last rightward. So vertex i could be visited after t_i. To avoid this complication, choose the last crossing of edge i in either direction? Let's define L_i = last time the walk is at vertex i? Hmm.

Alternative proof via edge formula and dynamic programming: The minimal sum E with parity and E_i>=1 can be solved by a greedy that chooses endpoint n-2/n-1 and yields recurrence. We can show recurrence matches the optimal solution of the edge constraints. Let's try to prove recurrence from edge constraints.

For a fixed endpoint epos, constraints are:
E_i parity = 1 if i<epos else 0, E_i >=1.
Vertex constraints:
E_0 >= 2a_0 -1 - [epos=0]
E_{n-2} >= 2a_{n-1} - [epos=n-1]
For 1<=i<=n-2: E_{i-1}+E_i >= 2a_i - [epos=i].

We want minimize sum E_i. This is a chain with parity. There is a known greedy: process from left to right, choose E_i as small as possible given parity and previous E_{i-1} to satisfy vertex i. At the end, choose endpoint to minimize. The recurrence with k_i is exactly this greedy for endpoint n-2 or n-1? Let's see.

For endpoint n-1 (all E_i odd):
Process i=0: E0 odd >=2a0-1. Choose minimal odd E0 = 2a0-1 (since need positive). This corresponds to k0 = a0-1, E0=2k0+1.
For vertex i (1<=i<=n-2): E_{i-1}+E_i >= 2a_i. Given E_{i-1}, choose minimal odd E_i >=1 satisfying. This yields E_i = max(1, 2a_i - E_{i-1}) adjusted to odd. The recurrence cur = k_{i-1}+1? Let's map. E_{i-1}=2k_{i-1}+1. visits to i before bounces at i = k_{i-1}+1 = cur. Need bounces k_i = max(0, a_i - cur). Then E_i = 2k_i+1. This is exactly greedy. At the end, E_{n-2} chosen to satisfy vertex n-2; vertex n-1 constraint E_{n-2} >= 2a_{n-1}-1. Greedy's final handling chooses E_{n-2} = 2k_{n-2}+1 (if move to last) or 2k_{n-2} (if stop at n-2)? Wait for endpoint n-1, E_{n-2} odd. If after satisfying n-2 with k_{n-2} bounces, visits to n-1 = k_{n-2}. If k_{n-2} >= a_{n-1}, then E_{n-2} could be 2k_{n-2} (even) and endpoint n-2, not n-1. If k_{n-2} < a_{n-1}, we need E_{n-2} odd at least? Let's derive.

Greedy at i=n-2:
Before bounces, cur = visits[n-2] = k_{n-3}+1.
If cur < a_{n-2}, k_{n-2}=a_{n-2}-cur bounces across edge n-2. Each bounce contributes 2 to E_{n-2}. After bounces, E_{n-2} = 2k_{n-2} (if no move to last yet). Visits to n-1 = k_{n-2}.
If k_{n-2} >= a_{n-1}, we can stop at n-2, so E_{n-2}=2k_{n-2} even, endpoint n-2.
If k_{n-2} < a_{n-1}, we move to n-1 (adds 1 to E_{n-2}, so E_{n-2}=2k_{n-2}+1 odd), visits n-1 = k_{n-2}+1. If still < a_{n-1}, do extra bounces across n-2: each adds 2 to E_{n-2} and one visit to n-1. So final E_{n-2} = 2k_{n-2}+1 + 2*(a_{n-1}-k_{n-2}-1) = 2a_{n-1}-1. Thus E_{n-2} odd = max? Actually if k_{n-2} >= a_{n-1}, E=2k_{n-2} even. If k_{n-2} < a_{n-1}, E=2a_{n-1}-1 odd. This is exactly the minimal E_{n-2} satisfying both vertex n-2 and n-1 constraints for endpoint n-2/n-1? Let's check edge constraints for endpoint n-2 (E_{n-2} even) require E_{n-2} >= 2a_{n-1} (since c_{n-1}=E_{n-2}/2 >= a_{n-1}) and E_{n-2} >= 2a_{n-2} -? For vertex n-2, e_{n-2}=1, constraint E_{n-3}+E_{n-2}+1 >= 2a_{n-2} => E_{n-3}+E_{n-2} >= 2a_{n-2}-1. Greedy's E_{n-2}=2k_{n-2} where k_{n-2}=max(0, a_{n-2} - cur). Since E_{n-3}=2k_{n-3}+1, cur=k_{n-3}+1. Then E_{n-3}+E_{n-2} = 2k_{n-3}+1 + 2max(0,a_{n-2}-k_{n-3}-1). If cur>=a, E=2k_{n-3}+1, sum >=2a-1? yes. If cur<a, sum = 2k+1+2(a-k-1)=2a-1. So minimal. Then if E_{n-2} < 2a_{n-1}, we switch to endpoint n-1 with E odd = 2a_{n-1}-1. But is this always minimal compared to increasing even E to satisfy last? We compared: moving to last (odd) is cheaper by 1 than doing extra even bounces. So yes.

For i < n-2, choosing E_i odd minimal given previous is optimal because endpoint must be to the right of i (since all vertices need positive and we eventually go to n-1; but if endpoint could be left of i, E_i even. However, we argued endpoint left of n-2 not better. In edge formula, for i < n-2, E_i odd in greedy. Could an optimal with endpoint epos <= i have E_i even and smaller sum? Let's compare. There is a known result: For a path with positive demands on all vertices, an optimal walk can be assumed to end at n-2 or n-1. Because if it ends at e < n-2, consider the last time it leaves e to the right? Actually after the last visit to n-1, it returns left to e. The segment from the last visit to n-1 back to e traverses edges n-2..e leftward. Removing the final return from e+1 to e? Wait if end at e, the last move is from e+1 to e. If we instead stop at e+1, we save one move and still have visited all vertices? But does e+1 have enough visits? The final move to e added a visit to e, which might be needed. If e's quota was already satisfied before, then stopping at e+1 saves a move. If e's quota was not satisfied before, then the final return provided a needed visit to e. But could that visit have been provided earlier more cheaply? The final return from e+1 to e costs 1 and gives one visit to e. To provide that visit earlier, you would need an extra round trip e<->e+1 costing 2, or maybe the initial left-to-right pass already gave one visit to e. Since need_e >=1, the initial pass gave one visit. If need_e >1, extra visits require round trips. The final return can provide one extra visit at cost1 (instead of cost2) if you were going to be at e+1 anyway at the end. So ending left can save 1 move for satisfying e's last extra visit. But it costs the return from e+1 to e, which is that 1 move; compared to ending at e+1, you spend 1 move and get a visit to e. If that visit is needed, it's not wasted. Could this make ending left strictly better than ending right? Let's find a concrete case.

But note: `prev_k` represents k at previous vertex (i-1). At vertex i, visits to i = prev_k + 1. Is this always true? At i=0, prev_k=0, visits0=1. At i=1, prev_k=k0, visits1=k0+1. Yes. So we can use `prev_k`.

Let's write can with `prev_k`:
```
def can(x):
    if x == 0: return True
    moves = 1
    prev_k = 0
    last_need = (x + points[-1] - 1)//points[-1]
    for i in range(n-1):
        cur = prev_k + 1
        need = (x + points[i] - 1)//points[i]
        if cur < need:
            k = need - cur
            moves += 2*k
            if moves > m: return False
        else:
            k = 0
        if i == n-2:
            if k >= last_need:
                return moves <= m
            moves += 1
            if moves > m: return False
            if k + 1 < last_need:
                moves += 2 * (last_need - k - 1)
                if moves > m: return False
            return True
        else:
            moves += 1
            if moves > m: return False
            prev_k = k
    return moves <= m
```
This is elegant. Let's test need [2,3,2]:

    need = [(mid + p - 1)//p for p in points]
    cnt = 0

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the recurrence if `cur` at vertex i is greater than need_i and we set k=0, but maybe we should do some bounces at i to help i+1 even though i is satisfied, because it could reduce total moves? We compared and found moving first is at least as good. Let's test with a concrete case where i+1 need high, i+2 need low. Suppose n=4, need [1,1,100,1]. Greedy:
i0 move to1 moves2 prev0.
i1 cur1 need1 k0 move to2 moves3 prev0.
i2 cur1 need100 k99 moves201, last_need1, k=99>=1 stop at2. Total201.

What if i+2 need high and i+1 need low, doing bounces at i+1 before moving to i+2 is equivalent to doing them after moving? Actually after moving to i+2, to bounce with i+2 you'd bounce i+2<->i+1, which also visits i+1. Cost same. So no difference. Greedy okay.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the recurrence if `prev_k` is large and `cur` at next vertex is large, so k=0, and then prev_k becomes0, meaning no bounces at next vertex. But what if next vertex need is low but the vertex after next need high; by not bouncing at next, we don't help the vertex after next. Could bouncing at next (even though next satisfied) help the vertex after next and reduce total moves? This is the same question. Suppose need [1,1,100,1] n=4, at i=1 (vertex1) need low, vertex2 need high. Greedy didn't bounce at1, then bounced at2. Alternative bounce at1 helped vertex2, total same. So no improvement. In general, bouncing at i helps i+1; if i+1 need high, it can reduce k at i+1. The total cost is same because each visit to i+1 costs 2 regardless of whether the bounce is across edge i (before moving) or across edge i+1 (after moving), except the move from i to i+1 gives a free visit. Let's formalize: To add one extra visit to vertex j (i+1) beyond the free arrival, you must traverse an edge incident to j twice. If you bounce with left neighbor, cost2 and visits j-1; if bounce with right neighbor, cost2 and visits j+1. Since j+1 also needs visits eventually, right bounce is not worse. Thus no need to bounce left when j is satisfied. Greedy always bounces right when needed. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the code if `m` is 0 (not) and can
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxScore(self, points: List[int], m: int) -> int:
        n = len(points)

        def can(x: int) -> bool:
            if x == 0:
                return True

            moves = 1  # first move to index 0
            prev_k = 0
            last_need = (x + points[-1] - 1) // points[-1]

            for i in range(n - 1):
                cur = prev_k + 1
                need = (x + points[i] - 1) // points[i]

                if cur < need:
                    k = need - cur
                    moves += 2 * k
                    if moves > m:
                        return False
                else:
                    k = 0

                if i == n - 2:
                    if k >= last_need:
                        return moves <= m

                    moves += 1
                    if moves > m:
                        return False

                    if k + 1 < last_need:
                        moves += 2 * (last_need - k - 1)
                        if moves > m:
                            return False
                    return True
                else:
                    moves += 1
                    if moves > m:
                        return False
                    prev_k = k

            return moves <= m

        lo, hi = 0, max(points) * m
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi + 1) // 2
            if can(mid):
                lo = mid
            else:
                hi = mid - 1

        return lo
```
